metrics = {'T_median': 80.0, 'T_median_ok': True, 'T_median_ratio': 1.0, 'contaminated_files': {}, ...}
submitted_data_stats = {'T_max': 80, 'T_mean': 80.0, 'T_median': 80.0, 'T_min': 80, ...}
reference_data_stats = {'chance_majority': {'choice': 0.4275624698836649, 'early_lick': 0.8850829489915331, 'outcome': 0.6843670406828664, 't...position': 0.25}, 'data_summary': {'T_max': 80, 'T_mean': 80.0, 'T_median': 80.0, 'T_min': 80, ...}, 'errors': [], ...}

    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Check the converted dataset matches the reference in size, variables and ranges.
    
        Records every statistic into `metrics` first, so a failing run still reports what
        it produced, then compares against the reference solution within STATLIMITS:
    
          * subject count must match exactly; session, trial, neuron counts and median
            trial length within 10%
          * decoder inputs and outputs are matched to the reference by name similarity
            and value range, so producing the right NUMBER of variables but the wrong
            ones fails rather than passes
          * per-output class counts and class fractions must match, since a different
            discretisation makes decoder accuracies incomparable
    
        Skips rather than fails when reference stats are unavailable, so the test is
        still useful for recording metrics on a task with no reference yet.
        """
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi))+1
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
            # Verdict recorded where the threshold is applied; the assert below reads it back.
            # The assert loop bails on its first failure, so computing these here is what makes
            # a later field's result knowable after an earlier one has failed.
            metrics[field+'_ok'] = bool(
                ratio_within_limits(metrics[field+'_ratio'], STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']))
    
        # Seed a per-variable verdict for every REFERENCE output, so a variable the agent never
        # produced -- and which therefore never appears in output_matches below -- counts as a
        # miss rather than shrinking the denominator of the fraction categories.
        reference_output_names = list(reference_data_stats['data_summary'].get('output_range') or [])
        for ref_name in reference_output_names:
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        # Named for what it is: the mean Hungarian MATCHING cost, which is dominated
        # by name similarity and is forced to 0 for exactly-matching names before any
        # range term is computed. It is not a mean of the input_range_error_<var>
        # fields -- that is mean_input_range_error, recorded below.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_match_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            scale = abs(ref_hi - ref_lo)
            if scale < 1e-6 and abs(sub_hi - sub_lo) < 1e-6:
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = 0.0
            else:
                scale = max(scale, 1e-6)
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale))
        mean_input_range_error = np.mean([metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] for ref_name, _, _ in input_matches]) if len(input_matches) else 0.0
        metrics['mean_input_range_error'] = mean_input_range_error
        # Recorded ONLY when there is something to match. A task with no decoder inputs
        # (allen2p: dinput == 0, input_range == {}) has no input ranges to compare, so leaving
        # the key unset scores the category undefined and drops it from the mean -- rather than
        # awarding a free 1.0 for a check that never happened. Matches undefined_categories()
        # in lesion_analysis.py, which singles out this same case. The assert below is guarded
        # by the same condition, so it never reads a key that was not written.
        if len(input_matches):
            metrics['input_range_ok'] = bool(
                mean_input_range_error <= STATLIMITS['input_range_error'])
    
        # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names
        output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
        )
        metrics['output_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in output_matches
        ]
    
        # As above: the mean matching cost, not a mean of output_fraction_error_<var>.
        mean_output_match_cost = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
        metrics['output_match_mean_cost'] = mean_output_match_cost
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']
        ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(abs(sub_hi - ref_hi))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the
            # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi) + 1)
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi) + 1)
            # fraction error per output
            ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())
            sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())
            if len(ref_fracs) == len(sub_fracs):
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))
            else:
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None
    
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] <= STATLIMITS['output_range_error'])
            # A None error means the class counts differ, so there is no comparable fraction
            # vector. That scores as a miss, but is NOT asserted on below -- the nclasses check
            # already fails that case, and asserting here too would change what the suite
            # rejects. Verdict and assert diverge only here, deliberately.
            frac_error = metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}']
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                frac_error is not None and frac_error <= STATLIMITS['output_fraction_error'])
    
        for field in fields_check:
>           assert metrics[field+'_ok'], f"{field} in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats[field]}) is not within {STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']*100}% of reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]})"
E           AssertionError: nsessions in submitted data (150) is not within 10.0% of reference (173)

This requires examining the provided papers, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n     9→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    10→- We will assess your work in two ways. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n    11→\n    12→## Reference Information\n    13→\n    14→- **Papers**:\n    15→  - Data were first presented in \"Brain-wide neural activity underlying memory-guided movement\". It is in the file `/app/datapaper.pdf`\n    16→  - A recent paper that uses this dataset is \"Brain-wide analysis reveals movement encoding structured across and within brain areas\". It is in the file `/app/methodpaper.pdf`\n    17→  - A white paper about spike sorting and quality control is in the file `/app/ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`\n    18→- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n    19→- **Code**:\n    20→  - `/app/code` contains code from `/app/methodpaper.pdf`.\n    21→- **Data** from the papers are in the directory `/app/data`.\n    22→\n    23→## Decoder Task\n    24→\n    25→- Temporally align based on **Go cue onset**. \n    26→- Extract **2.5 s before to 1.5 s after** the go cue for each trial\n    27→- Use **50-ms-width bins** for computing firing rates. \n    28→\n    29→### Decoder Inputs:\n    30→- Time from **tone onset** in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n    31→- Whether **photostimulation** is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying)\n    32→\n    33→### Decoder Outputs\n    34→- Lick direction **choice** (left, right, no lick, per-trial)\n    35→- **Outcome** (ignore, miss, hit, per-trial)\n    36→- **Early lick** (no, yes, per-trial)\n    37→- **Tongue y-position**, per-session discretization:\n    38→  - 0: < 40th percentile of y-position over the session\n    39→  - 1: 40th to 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n    40→  - 2: > 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n    41→  - 3: not visible\n    42→\n    43→## Target Data Format\n    44→\n    45→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n    46→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    47→\n    48→```python\n    49→data = {\n    50→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    51→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    52→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    53→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    54→            ...\n    55→        ],\n    56→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    57→            ...\n    58→        ],\n    59→        ...\n    60→    ],\n    61→\n    62→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    63→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    64→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    65→            ...\n    66→        ],\n    67→        ...\n    68→    ],\n    69→\n    70→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    71→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    72→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    73→            ...\n    74→        ],\n    75→        ...\n    76→    ],\n    77→\n    78→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    79→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    80→\n    81→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    82→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    83→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    84→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    85→\n    86→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    87→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    88→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    89→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    90→        ...\n    91→    ],\n    92→\n    93→    'metadata': {\n    94→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n    95→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n    96→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n    97→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    98→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    99→        ...\n   100→    }\n   101→}\n   102→```\n   103→\n   104→### Key Format Requirements\n   105→\n   106→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   107→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   108→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   109→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   110→\n   111→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   112→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   113→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   114→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   115→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   116→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   117→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   118→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   119→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   120→\n   121→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   122→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   123→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   124→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   125→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   126→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   127→\n   128→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   129→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   130→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   131→\n   132→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   133→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   134→\n   135→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   136→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   137→\n   138→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   139→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   140→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   141→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   142→\n   143→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   144→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   145→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   146→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   147→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   148→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   149→\n   150→## Decoder Reference\n   151→\n   152→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   153→- Optional arguments:\n   154→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   155→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   156→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   157→\n   158→### Purpose\n   159→\n   160→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   161→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   162→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   163→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   164→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   165→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   166→\n   167→### Consistency requirements\n   168→\n   169→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n   170→- Loading of data\n   171→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   172→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   173→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   174→\n   175→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n   176→\n   177→## Success Criteria\n   178→\n   179→A successful conversion should:\n   180→1. All required files must be created:\n   181→  - `/app/convert_data.py`\n   182→  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\n   183→2. Match the target data structure exactly\n   184→3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n   185→4. Include all relevant data from the source\n   186→5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\n   187→6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\n   188→  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\n   189→  - Decoder accuracy\n   190→7. names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. All required files must be created:\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\n  - Decoder accuracy\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. Your code must implement the decisions as you describe them.\n","numLines":191,"startLine":1,"totalLines":191}}}

How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n     4→\n     5→i. The dataset is distributed as one NWB file per session under `data/sub-<subject_id>/`. All sessions are found with a single glob over that layout, and each file is opened with `pynwb` and processed once.
