Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\\\\n\\\\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\\\\n\\\\nProbes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\\\\n\\\\nPhotoinhibition\\\\n\\\\nLight from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\\\\n\\\\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\\\\ntime window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\\\\n\\\\nSpike sorting and quality control\\\\n\\\\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\\\\n\\\\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2. \\\\n\\\", \\\"numLines\\\": 29, \\\"startLine\\\": 1, \\\"totalLines\\\": 29}}\"\n   436→          }\n   437→        ]\n   438→      },\n   439→      \"extra\": {\n   440→        \"stop_reason\": \"tool_use\",\n   441→        \"cwd\": \"/app\",\n   442→        \"is_sidechain\": false,\n   443→        \"tool_use_name\": \"Read\",\n   444→        \"tool_result_metadata\": {\n   445→          \"tool_use_result\": {\n   446→            \"type\": \"text\",\n   447→            \"file\": {\n   448→              \"filePath\": \"/app/methods.txt\",\n   449→              \"content\": \"Behavior and video tracking\\n\\nMice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\\nquency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\\nresponse trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\\n\\nTwo CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\\n\\nMulti-regional electrophysiological recordings\\n\\nWe designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \\u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\\n\\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\\n\\nProbes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\\n\\nPhotoinhibition\\n\\nLight from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\\n\\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\\ntime window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\\n\\nSpike sorting and quality control\\n\\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\\n\\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\\n\\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2. \\n\",\n   467→              \"numLines\": 29,\n   468→              \"startLine\": 1,\n   469→              \"totalLines\": 29\n   470→            }\n   471→          },\n   472→          \"raw_tool_result\": {\n   473→            \"tool_use_id\": \"toolu_0199xdchbGUvzhteT2XsAmAQ\",\n   474→            \"type\": \"tool_result\",\n   475→            \"content\": \"     1→Behavior and video tracking\\n     2→\\n     3→Mice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\\n     4→quency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\\n     5→response trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\\n     6→\\n     7→Two CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. The last sample_start before the go cue is the one that matters - that's when the tone actually started for that trial.\"\n> \"time_from_tone_onset, computed as the bin center minus the tone onset relative to go (a negative offset near -1.85s)\"\n\n**Implementation (step 83, lines 239-249, 283):**\n- Default tone onset relative to go = -1.85s (fallback)\n- For each trial, finds the last `sample_start_time` before the go cue using `np.searchsorted`\n- Computes `tone_onset_rel_go = last_sample_start - go_time`\n- Time from tone at each bin = `BIN_CENTERS - tone_onset_rel_go` (continuous, time-varying)\n\n---\n\n## 7. How It Handled Photostimulation\n\n**Step 36 (reasoning_content):**\n> \"photostim_onset is stored as strings ('N/A' or numeric strings)\"\n> \"If photostim_onset is relative to trial start and go_time ~= trial_start + presample(0.5s) + sample(0.65s) + delay(1.2s) ~= trial_start + 2.35s, then a photostim_onset of 2.522 would fall about 0.17s before the go cue -- consistent with the late delay window. How It Derived Choice, Outcome, Early_lick\n\n**Step 31 (reasoning_content):**\n> \"Thinking through 'choice' as an output: trial_instruction gives the correct direction while outcome (hit/miss/ignore) tells us if the animal licked correctly, incorrectly, or not at all, so I can derive choice by combining instruction with outcome -- hit means choice matches instruction, miss means choice is the opposite direction, and ignore means no lick.\"\n\n**Step 48 (reasoning_content):**\n> \"I'm now working out the mapping between instructed direction, outcome, and actual choice -- deriving 'left'/'right'/'no lick' from hit/miss/ignore combined with instruction side\"\n\n**Implementation (step 83, lines 287-301):**\n- Choice: `ignore` -> `no_lick` (2); `hit` -> same as instruction (left=0, right=1); `miss` -> opposite of instruction; else -> `no_lick` (2)\n- Outcome: mapped as `{'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}`\n- Early lick: `1 if early_licks[t_i] == 'early' else 0`\n- All outputs are replicated across all 80 time bins (time-varying format) except tongue_y which is genuinely time-varying\n\n---\n\n## 9. No smoothing, normalization, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_EDGES = np.linspace(T_START, T_END, N_BINS + 1)\n...\ndef compute_firing_rates_all(spike_times_list, go_cue_times):\n    n_units = len(spike_times_list)\n    n_trials = len(go_cue_times)\n    fr_all = np.zeros((n_units, n_trials, N_BINS), dtype=np.float32)\n    for u_i, spike_times in enumerate(spike_times_list):\n        st = np.sort(spike_times)\n        for t_i in range(n_trials):\n            go = go_cue_times[t_i]\n            i_lo = np.searchsorted(st, go + T_START, side='left')\n            i_hi = np.searchsorted(st, go + T_END, side='left')\n            if i_hi > i_lo:\n                rel = st[i_lo:i_hi] - go\n                counts, _ = np.histogram(rel, bins=BIN_EDGES)\n                fr_all[u_i, t_i] = counts / BIN_WIDTH\n    return fr_all\n```\n\niii. The agent used `np.histogram` for spike counting within each trial window, consistent with the 50ms bin width specified in the instructions.\n\n## 2-c. No smoothing, normalization, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_EDGES = np.linspace(T_START, T_END, N_BINS + 1)\n...\ndef compute_firing_rates_all(spike_times_list, go_cue_times):\n    n_units = len(spike_times_list)\n    n_trials = len(go_cue_times)\n    fr_all = np.zeros((n_units, n_trials, N_BINS), dtype=np.float32)\n    for u_i, spike_times in enumerate(spike_times_list):\n        st = np.sort(spike_times)\n        for t_i in range(n_trials):\n            go = go_cue_times[t_i]\n            i_lo = np.searchsorted(st, go + T_START, side='left')\n            i_hi = np.searchsorted(st, go + T_END, side='left')\n            if i_hi > i_lo:\n                rel = st[i_lo:i_hi] - go\n                counts, _ = np.histogram(rel, bins=BIN_EDGES)\n                fr_all[u_i, t_i] = counts / BIN_WIDTH\n    return fr_all\n```\n\niii. The agent used `np.histogram` for spike counting within each trial window, consistent with the 50ms bin width specified in the instructions.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `classification == 'good'` are kept. No additional quality metric thresholds are applied. Sessions with no good units are dropped.\n\nii.\n```python\nclassifications = units['classification'][:]\ngood_idx = np.where(classifications == 'good')[0]\nif len(good_idx) == 0:\n    io.close()\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The agent identified `classification` as the QC classifier verdict from the spike sorting quality control paper.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Spike times and go cue times are on the same session-absolute clock. Each trial's bin edges are computed as `go_cue + BIN_EDGES`, and spikes are histogrammed into those bins.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo = go_cue_times[t_i]\ni_lo = np.searchsorted(st, go + T_START, side='left')\ni_hi = np.searchsorted(st, go + T_END, side='left')\nrel = st[i_lo:i_hi] - go\ncounts, _ = np.histogram(rel, bins=BIN_EDGES)\n```\n\niii. The agent recognized that all NWB times share a global clock, so alignment to go cue only requires looking up each trial's go cue time.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The time bin size is 50ms. Spike times are binned into 80 non-overlapping 50ms bins spanning -2.5s to +1.5s relative to the go cue. The bin grid is defined once and reused for every trial and session.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_WIDTH = 0.05  # 50 ms\nT_START = -2.5\nT_END = 1.5\nN_BINS = int(round((T_END - T_START) / BIN_WIDTH))  # 80\nBIN_EDGES = np.linspace(T_START, T_END, N_BINS + 1)\nBIN_CENTERS = (BIN_EDGES[:-1] + BIN_EDGES[1:]) / 2.0\n```\n\niii. The window and 50ms bin width are set by the instructions. No rebinning is applied beyond the initial binning from spike times.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* derived from?\n\ni. From `sample_start_times` (tone onsets) in BehavioralEvents, together with the go cue of each trial. {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_1","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,260p' /tests/reference_DECISIONS.md\"","aggregated_output":"# Decisions\n\n## 1-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The dataset is distributed as one NWB file per session under `data/sub-<subject_id>/`. All sessions are found with a single glob over that layout, and each file is opened with `pynwb` and processed once. Subjects, trials, and units are then read from within each file (`nwb.subject`, `nwb.trials`, `nwb.units`, `nwb.acquisition`).\n\nii. Finding all data:\n```python\nfiles = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n...\nfor i, path in enumerate(files, 1):\n    res = process_session(path)\n```\n\nLoading one session:\n```python\nwith NWBHDF5IO(path, mode='r', load_namespaces=True) as io:\n    nwb = io.read()\n    units = nwb.units\n    trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n    bev = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents'].time_series\n```\n\niii. NWB is the published format for this dataset and `pynwb` is its standard reader. Since there is one file per session, the directory listing is the complete set of sessions and a glob is sufficient; sorting it makes the session order deterministic. The resulting counts (174 files, 28 subjects) match `assetsSummary` in `data/dandiset.yaml`.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. Each NWB file records its animal in `nwb.subject.subject_id`, a numeric string such as `'440956'`. That value is read for every session and carried through to assembly, where `subjects` is the sorted set of unique ids and `subject_idx` gives each session's index into that list.\n\nii. Per session:\n```python\nreturn {\n    'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n    'subject': nwb.subject.subject_id,\n    ...\n```\n\nAt assembly:\n```python\nsubjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\nsub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n...\n'subjects': subjects,\n'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n```\n\niii. `subject_id` is the canonical animal identifier in the file, and the containing folder name (`sub-440956`) is derived from it, so no separate grouping step is needed. This gives 28 subjects with 3-10 sessions each, matching the dandiset. The numeric id differs from the mouse name used in the papers — `nwb.identifier` is e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, so subject `440956` is mouse `SC015` — but the numeric id is what the NWB subject field provides, so it is used directly.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. One NWB file is one session, so no grouping or splitting is needed. Each session is identified by `nwb.identifier` (e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, encoding mouse, date, time and session number), recorded per session and also listed in `metadata['session_info']`. Session order in the output follows the sorted file list.\n\nii.\n```python\nfiles = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n```\n\n```python\n'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n```\n\n```python\n'session_info': [\n    {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'],\n     'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n    for r in results\n],\n```\n\niii. The dandiset already stores one session per file, so the file boundary is the session boundary and nothing has to be inferred. Because the filename embeds the acquisition timestamp (`sub-440956_ses-20190207T120657_...`), sorting the paths puts sessions in chronological order within each subject. 173 of the 174 files reach the output; the one exception is dropped for having no quality-controlled units (see 2-c).\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. Trials come from the NWB trials table (`nwb.trials`), one row per behavioural trial, with exactly one go-cue event per row. The row count is checked against the number of go-cue events.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\ngo = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\nassert len(go) == len(trials), f'{len(go)} go cues vs {len(trials)} trials'\n```\n\niii. Trials are clearly defined by the trials table, so it is used directly rather than re-deriving boundaries from the event streams. `go_start_times` has exactly one event per trial in all 174 sessions, which makes the mapping unambiguous. This is not true of the other trial-phase events: `sample_start_times` and `delay_start_times` can have several entries per trial, because a lick during the sample or delay replays that epoch.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials are filtered only for absence of spike data, in two cases. First, trials outside `units/obs_intervals`: in 8 sessions the ephys recording starts after the behaviour, leaving up to 376 leading trials with no spikes. Second, `free_water` trials, which have no spikes anywhere in the window even though `obs_intervals` covers them. A session is dropped entirely if fewer than 2 trials survive. No behavioural quality filter is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\n# filter behavioral trials with no spike data\noi_off = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].data)\noi_start = np.concatenate([[0], oi_off[:-1]])\nobs = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].target.data)[oi_start[good[0]]:oi_off[good[0]]]\nkeep = np.isin(np.round(trials['start_time'].values, 4), np.round(obs[:, 0], 4))\nassert keep.sum() == len(obs), f'{keep.sum()} matched vs {len(obs)} observed'\n\n# filter no water trials\nkeep &= trials['free_water'].values == 0\nif keep.sum() < 2:\n    return None\ntrials = trials[keep]\ngo = go[keep]\n```\n\niii. `obs_intervals` is the file's own record of which trials were observed, and its start times match `trials.start_time`, which makes the mapping exact. The `free_water` exclusion was found empirically — those trials are covered by `obs_intervals` yet contain no spikes at all — and accounts for 2,449 of the 2,451 all-zero trials remaining after the `obs_intervals` filter.\n\nEarly-lick and no-response (`ignore`) trials are deliberately kept, even though the data paper states \"Early lick trials and no response trials were excluded for analysis\", because both are required decoder outputs here. The 2-trial minimum is the target format's requirement.\n\nTogether these remove 3,510 of the 94,370 trials in the retained sessions (3.7%), leaving 90,860.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `units/spike_times`, the sorted spike times of each unit in session-absolute seconds. Only units with `classification == 'good'` contribute (see 2-c). The go-cue times (`BehavioralEvents/go_start_times`) are the other input, used to place the bin edges.\n\nii.\n```python\noffs = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].data)\nallst = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].target.data)\nstarts = np.concatenate([[0], offs[:-1]])\n\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n```\n\niii. `spike_times` is the only neural representation in the file, so firing rates are computed from it directly.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times are converted to per-bin firing rates in Hz. For each good unit, the bin edges for every trial are built as one flat array of absolute times, `np.searchsorted` gives the running spike count at each edge, and differencing adjacent counts gives the spike count per bin. Counts are divided by the bin width to give Hz. No smoothing, normalisation, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n    # running spike total at each edge; differencing gives the count per bin\n    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\nrates /= BIN                                  # counts -> Hz\n```\n\niii. Firing rate is computed from the binned spike count over the 50 ms window defined in the instructions. This also matches the reference code, which calls `sliding_histogram(..., rate=True)` and returns `binSpikes / bin_width`.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `units/classification == 'good'` are kept. No thresholds are applied to any individual quality metric. A session with no such units is dropped entirely. This retains 69,453 of 272,227 units (25.5%), a median of 390 per session.\n\nii.\n```python\n# ---- units: QC classifier verdict ----------------------------------\ncls = _text(units['classification'])\ngood = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\nif good.size == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. `classification` is the verdict of the spike-sorting quality-control classifier described in `ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`.\n\n`units/unit_quality` ('good' / 'multi') is deliberately not used: it is an older label that disagrees on 12.2% of the classifier-good units and is far more permissive.\n\nThe one dropped session (`sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508`) has `classification` and `anno_name` set to NaN for all 1,852 units, i.e. it was never quality-controlled. Excluding it gives 173 sessions and 69,453 good units, against the white paper's 173 sessions and 69,943 units.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Spike times and event times are already on the same session-absolute clock, so no alignment step is needed. The bin edges relative to the go cue are added to each trial's go-cue time to give the absolute time window for that trial, and the spikes are binned against those edges directly.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n```\n\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n```\n\niii. Everything in the NWB file is timestamped on one global clock, so aligning to the go cue only requires looking up each trial's go-cue time and taking the window around it. There is no resampling or interpolation, and no per-stream offset to correct.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into 80 non-overlapping 50 ms bins spanning -2.5 s to +1.5 s relative to the go cue. The bin grid is defined once, as 81 edges relative to the go cue, and reused for every trial and session, so every trial has the same 80 timepoints.\n\nii.\n```python\n# trial window relative to the go cue, and bin width, in seconds\nT_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\nBIN = 0.05\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n\nREL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n```\n\niii. The window and the 50 ms bin width are set by the instructions. Defining the grid once as offsets from the go cue gives a constant 80 timepoints per trial, which the target format requires.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* derived from?\n\ni. From `sample_start_times`, the tone onsets of the session, together with the go cue of each trial. The tone taken for a trial is the **last one** before its go cue.\n\nii. The tone of each trial:\n```python\nsample = np.asarray(bev['sample_start_times'].timestamps)\ntone = sample[np.searchsorted(sample, go, side='left') - 1]\n```\n\niii. An early lick replays the sample epoch, so a trial can carry more than one tone; the last one before the go cue is the one the animal actually used.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_from_tone_onset*?\n\ni. The bins sit around the go cue, so the value of a bin is its center plus the gap between the tone and the go cue.\n\nii. The bin center, and the shift that turns them into time from the tone:\n```python\nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n```python\n# bins sit around the go cue, values are seconds since the tone\ntime_from_tone = CENTERS[None, :] + (go - tone)[:, None]\n```\n\niii. No additional processing needed other than find the tone onset time.\n\n## 3-c. How is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is how the neural binnning grid is defined.\n\nii. The grid, defined once as offsets from the go cue, and laid on each trial's go cue to bin the spikes:\n```python\nT_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\nBIN = 0.05\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n\nREL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *photostim* derived from?\n\ni. From `photostim_onset` and `photostim_duration` in the trials table, with `start_time` and the go cue used to place them on the trial's time axis.\n\nii. The onset and offset of the stimulation, relative to the go cue:\n```python\non_s = trials['photostim_onset'].values\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

The oracle arm runs\n# the script from /solution while the dataset is mounted read-only at /app/data, so\n# solve.sh overrides this with --datadir.\nDATA_DIR = os.path.join(HERE, 'data')\n\n# trial window relative to the go cue, and bin width, in seconds\nT_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\nBIN = 0.05\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             # 80\n\nREL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         # 81 edges, re go cue\nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        # 80 bin centres\n\nOUTCOME_CODE = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\nEARLY_CODE = {'no early': 0, 'early': 1}\nSIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\nCHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\nTONGUE_HIDDEN = 3                # no visible tongue frame in the bin\n\nTONGUE_CONF = 0.5                # tracking likelihood for the tongue to count as visible\nTONGUE_PCT = (40, 60)            # per-session percentiles -> 3 visible classes\n\nINPUT_NAMES = ['time_from_tone_onset', 'photostim']\nOUTPUT_NAMES = ['choice', 'outcome', 'early_lick', 'tongue_y_position']\n\n# output_values[i][code] names each value of output i\nOUTPUT_VALUES = [\n    ['left', 'right', 'no lick'],\n    ['ignore', 'miss', 'hit'],\n    ['no', 'yes'],\n    ['below 40th pct', '40th to 60th pct', 'above 60th pct', 'not visible'],\n]\n\n\ndef region_label(anno):\n    \"\"\"'Orbital area, lateral part, layer 5' -> 'orbital area'.\"\"\"\n    return re.split(r'[,./]', anno)[0].strip().lower()\n\n\ndef _text(col):\n    \"\"\"Read a text column; non-str entries (unlabelled sessions) become ''.\"\"\"\n    return np.array([x if isinstance(x, str) else '' for x in col[:]])\n\n\ndef _bin_mean(idx, y, n):\n    \"\"\"Mean of y per bin given a per-sample bin index. NaN where a bin is empty.\"\"\"\n    ok = ~np.isnan(y)\n    tot = np.bincount(idx[ok], weights=y[ok], minlength=n)\n    cnt = np.bincount(idx[ok], minlength=n)\n    with np.errstate(invalid='ignore'):\n        return np.where(cnt > 0, tot / np.maximum(cnt, 1), np.nan)\n\n\ndef tongue_output(nwb, go):\n    \"\"\"Discretise tongue y into (n_trials, N_BINS) classes.\"\"\"\n    ts = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\n    t_cam = np.asarray(ts.timestamps)\n    data = ts.data[:]                        # (n_frames, 3) = x, y, likelihood\n    y = data[:, 1].copy()\n    y[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n\n    # bin the whole session on one grid, then take percentiles of the bin means\n    gidx = np.floor((t_cam - t_cam[0]) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n    edges = np.nanpercentile(_bin_mean(gidx, y, int(gidx[-1]) + 1), TONGUE_PCT)\n\n    # per trial, bin the same way and digitise against those edges\n    cls = np.full((len(go), N_BINS), TONGUE_HIDDEN, np.int8)\n    lo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\n    hi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\n    for i in range(len(go)):\n        b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n        np.clip(b, 0, N_BINS - 1, out=b)\n        m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n        ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n        cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n    return cls\n\n\ndef process_session(path):\n    \"\"\"Convert one NWB file. Returns a dict, or None if the session is dropped.\"\"\"\n    with NWBHDF5IO(path, mode='r', load_namespaces=True) as io:\n        nwb = io.read()\n        units = nwb.units\n        trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n        bev = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents'].time_series\n\n        # ---- units: QC classifier verdict ----------------------------------\n        cls = _text(units['classification'])\n        good = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\n        if good.size == 0:\n            return None\n        regions = [region_label(a) for a in _text(units['anno_name'])[good]]\n\n        # all NWB times are session-absolute seconds\n        go = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n        assert len(go) == len(trials), f'{len(go)} go cues vs {len(trials)} trials'\n\n        # ---- trials filtering ----\n        # spike data\n        # obs_intervals gives one [start, stop] per observed trial, which in some\n        # sessions is fewer than the behavioural trials. \n        # a behavioural trial is observed if its start time is one of the interval starts\n        obs = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'][good[0]])        \n        keep = np.isin(trials['start_time'].values, obs[:, 0])\n        assert keep.sum() == len(obs), f'{keep.sum()} matched vs {len(obs)} observed'\n\n        # filter no water trials\n        keep &= trials['free_water'].values == 0\n        \n        # a sessioon has to have at least 2 trials according to the instruction\n        if keep.sum() < 2:\n            return None\n        \n        trials = trials[keep]\n        go = go[keep]\n        n_trials = len(go)\n\n        # last tone onset before the go cue; an early lick replays the sample epoch\n        sample = np.asarray(bev['sample_start_times'].timestamps)\n        tone = sample[np.searchsorted(sample, go, side='left') - 1]\n\n        # ---- inputs --------------------------------------------------------\n        # bins sit around the go cue, values are seconds since the tone\n        time_from_tone = CENTERS[None, :] + (go - tone)[:, None]\n\n        # photostim onsets are stored as strings relative to trial start\n        on_s = trials['photostim_onset'].values\n        has = on_s != 'N/A'\n        stim_on = np.full(n_trials, np.nan)\n        stim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\n        stim_off = np.full(n_trials, np.nan)\n        stim_off[has] = stim_on[has] + trials['photostim_duration'].values[has].astype(float)\n        # NaN compares False, so non-stim trials stay 0\n        photostim = ((CENTERS[None, :] >= stim_on[:, None])\n                     & (CENTERS[None, :] < stim_off[:, None]))\n\n        inp = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim], axis=1).astype(np.float32)\n\n        # ---- outputs -------------------------------------------------------\n        # choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\n        outcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n        side = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\n        choice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n                          np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n\n        # per-trial values are repeated across bins so all outputs share one array\n        out = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n        out[:, 0, :] = choice[:, None]\n        out[:, 1, :] = np.array([OUTCOME_CODE[x] for x in outcome_s])[:, None]\n        out[:, 2, :] = np.array([EARLY_CODE[x] for x in trials['early_lick'].values])[:, None]\n        out[:, 3, :] = tongue_output(nwb, go)\n\n        # ---- neural --------------------------------------------------------\n        # spike_times is ragged: read the buffer once instead of one read per unit\n        offs = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].data)\n        allst = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].target.data)\n        starts = np.concatenate([[0], offs[:-1]])\n\n        edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n        rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n        for r, u in enumerate(good):\n            s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n            # running spike total at each edge; differencing gives the count per bin\n            pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n            rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n        rates /= BIN                                  # counts -> Hz\n\n        return {\n            'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n            'subject': nwb.subject.subject_id,\n            'neural': [np.ascontiguousarray(rates[:, t, :]) for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'input': [inp[t] for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'output': [out[t] for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'regions': regions,\n            'n_trials': n_trials,\n            'n_units': int(good.size),\n        }\n\n\ndef assemble(results):\n    \"\"\"Stitch per-session results into the target dictionary.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n\n    brain_regions = sorted({g for r in results for g in r['regions']})\n    reg_ix = {g: i for i, g in enumerate(brain_regions)}\n\n    return {\n        'neural': [r['neural'] for r in results],\n        'input': [r['input'] for r in results],\n        'output': [r['output'] for r in results],\n\n        'subjects': subjects,\n        'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n\n        'brain_regions': brain_regions,\n        'brain_region_idx': [np.array([reg_ix[g] for g in r['regions']], np.int32)\n                             for r in results],\n\n        'input_names': INPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_names': OUTPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_values': OUTPUT_VALUES,\n\n        'metadata': {\n            'task_description':\n                'Auditory delayed-response task. A 3 kHz or 12 kHz tone (0.65 s '\n                'sample epoch) instructs a left or right lick, followed by a 1.2 s '\n                'delay; a 6 kHz go cue opens a 1.5 s response window. Licking '\n                'during sample or delay replays that epoch. Decoded variables: '\n                'lick direction, outcome, early lick, tongue y-position.',\n            'time_bin_size': BIN * 1000.0,          # ms\n            'temporal_alignment_event': 'go cue onset (auditory, 6 kHz, 0.1 s)',\n            'off_start': T_START,\n            'off_end': T_STOP,\n\n            'dataset': 'DANDI 000363, Mesoscale Activity Map (Chen et al. 2023)',\n            'neural_units': 'Hz',\n            'unit_curation':\n                \"units/classification == 'good', the QC classifier of Chen, \"\n                'Colonell, Li & Svoboda (2023). No metric thresholds applied.',\n            'trial_curation':\n                'Trials without spike data excluded: outside units/obs_intervals, '\n                'or free_water.',\n            'tongue_discretisation':\n                'Side-camera tongue y, likelihood < %.2f discarded, survivors '\n                'averaged into %d ms bins; class edges are the %dth/%dth percentiles '\n                'of the session bin means. Bins with no visible frame get the '\n                'trailing \"not visible\" class.'\n                % (TONGUE_CONF, BIN * 1000, TONGUE_PCT[0], TONGUE_PCT[1]),\n            'session_info': [\n                {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'],\n                 'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n                for r in results\n            ],\n        },\n    }\n\n\ndef main():\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_4","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,260p' /app/convert_data.py\"","aggregated_output":"\"\"\"\nConvert MAP (Memory-guided Action Planning) NWB data to the standardized decoder format.\n\nData source: \"Brain-wide neural activity underlying memory-guided movement\" (Chen et al.)\nAnalysis reference: \"Brain-wide analysis reveals movement encoding structured across and within brain areas\"\n\nProcessing decisions:\n- Unit filtering: classification == 'good' (QC classifier approach from the paper)\n- Trial filtering: only trials within recording observation intervals are included\n- Session filtering: >65% correct on control (non-photostim, non-early-lick) trials,\n  and at least 50 correct lick-left and 50 correct lick-right control trials\n- Temporal alignment: Go cue onset (from BehavioralEvents/go_start_times)\n- Window: -2.5s to +1.5s around go cue\n- Bin width: 50ms non-overlapping bins -> firing rates (spikes/s)\n- Brain regions: derived from electrode_group location metadata (probe target region),\n  with left/right prefix removed\n- Tongue y-position: discretized per session from Camera0_side_TongueTracking,\n  using DLC likelihood threshold of 0.9 for visibility\n- All trial types included (early lick, ignore, miss, hit) since these are decoder outputs\n\"\"\"\n\nimport os\nimport glob\nimport json\nimport pickle\nimport numpy as np\nimport pynwb\n\n# Constants\nBIN_WIDTH = 0.05  # 50 ms\nT_START = -2.5    # seconds relative to go cue\nT_END = 1.5       # seconds relative to go cue\nN_BINS = int(round((T_END - T_START) / BIN_WIDTH))  # 80\nBIN_EDGES = np.linspace(T_START, T_END, N_BINS + 1)\nBIN_CENTERS = (BIN_EDGES[:-1] + BIN_EDGES[1:]) / 2.0\nTONGUE_LIKELIHOOD_THRESHOLD = 0.9\nMIN_CORRECT_PER_SIDE = 50\nMIN_PERFORMANCE = 0.65\n\n\ndef get_brain_region(electrode_group):\n    \"\"\"Extract simplified brain region name from electrode group location.\"\"\"\n    loc = json.loads(electrode_group.location)\n    region = loc.get('brain_regions', '')\n    for prefix in ['left ', 'right ']:\n        if region.startswith(prefix):\n            region = region[len(prefix):]\n    return region\n\n\ndef get_valid_trial_mask(obs_intervals, go_cue_times):\n    \"\"\"\n    Determine which trials have go cues within the observation intervals.\n    A trial is valid if its go cue time falls within any observation interval,\n    and the full extraction window [-2.5, 1.5] around the go cue is within\n    the observation range.\n    \"\"\"\n    n_trials = len(go_cue_times)\n    valid = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=bool)\n\n    for t_i in range(n_trials):\n        go = go_cue_times[t_i]\n        # Check if go cue falls within any obs interval\n        for obs_start, obs_end in obs_intervals:\n            if go >= obs_start and go <= obs_end:\n                valid[t_i] = True\n                break\n\n    return valid\n\n\ndef compute_firing_rates_all(spike_times_list, go_cue_times):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute firing rates for all units across given trials.\n    Returns array of shape (n_units, n_trials, N_BINS).\n    \"\"\"\n    n_units = len(spike_times_list)\n    n_trials = len(go_cue_times)\n    fr_all = np.zeros((n_units, n_trials, N_BINS), dtype=np.float32)\n\n    for u_i, spike_times in enumerate(spike_times_list):\n        if len(spike_times) == 0:\n            continue\n        st = np.sort(spike_times)\n        for t_i in range(n_trials):\n            go = go_cue_times[t_i]\n            i_lo = np.searchsorted(st, go + T_START, side='left')\n            i_hi = np.searchsorted(st, go + T_END, side='left')\n            if i_hi > i_lo:\n                rel = st[i_lo:i_hi] - go\n                counts, _ = np.histogram(rel, bins=BIN_EDGES)\n                fr_all[u_i, t_i] = counts / BIN_WIDTH\n    return fr_all\n\n\ndef discretize_tongue_y(tongue_timestamps, tongue_data, go_cue_times):\n    \"\"\"\n    Discretize tongue y-position per trial per time bin.\n    Returns: list of arrays, each shape (N_BINS,)\n    \"\"\"\n    tongue_y_vals = tongue_data[:, 1]\n    tongue_lk_vals = tongue_data[:, 2]\n    n_trials = len(go_cue_times)\n\n    # First pass: collect visible y values for percentiles\n    all_visible_y = []\n    for t_i in range(n_trials):\n        go = go_cue_times[t_i]\n        i_start = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, go + T_START, side='left')\n        i_end = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, go + T_END, side='left')\n        if i_end > i_start:\n            lk = tongue_lk_vals[i_start:i_end]\n            vis = lk >= TONGUE_LIKELIHOOD_THRESHOLD\n            if np.any(vis):\n                all_visible_y.append(tongue_y_vals[i_start:i_end][vis])\n\n    if len(all_visible_y) > 0:\n        all_vis = np.concatenate(all_visible_y)\n        p40 = np.percentile(all_vis, 40)\n        p60 = np.percentile(all_vis, 60)\n    else:\n        p40 = p60 = 0.0\n\n    # Second pass: discretize per bin\n    result = []\n    for t_i in range(n_trials):\n        go = go_cue_times[t_i]\n        ty_binned = np.full(N_BINS, 3, dtype=np.int64)\n        abs_edges = go + BIN_EDGES\n        edge_indices = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, abs_edges, side='left')\n\n        for b_i in range(N_BINS):\n            i_start = edge_indices[b_i]\n            i_end = edge_indices[b_i + 1]\n            if i_end > i_start:\n                lk = tongue_lk_vals[i_start:i_end]\n                vis = lk >= TONGUE_LIKELIHOOD_THRESHOLD\n                if np.any(vis):\n                    mean_y = np.mean(tongue_y_vals[i_start:i_end][vis])\n                    if mean_y < p40:\n                        ty_binned[b_i] = 0\n                    elif mean_y <= p60:\n                        ty_binned[b_i] = 1\n                    else:\n                        ty_binned[b_i] = 2\n        result.append(ty_binned)\n    return result\n\n\ndef process_session(nwb_path):\n    \"\"\"Process a single NWB session file. Returns dict or None if filtered.\"\"\"\n    io = pynwb.NWBHDF5IO(nwb_path, 'r')\n    nwb = io.read()\n\n    subject_id = str(nwb.subject.subject_id) if nwb.subject else 'unknown'\n    trials = nwb.trials\n    n_trials_total = len(trials)\n    units = nwb.units\n\n    # Get behavioral events\n    be = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents']\n    go_cue_times_all = be.time_series['go_start_times'].timestamps[:]\n    sample_start_times = be.time_series['sample_start_times'].timestamps[:]\n\n    if len(go_cue_times_all) != n_trials_total:\n        io.close()\n        return None\n\n    # Get trial info (all trials)\n    outcomes_all = trials['outcome'][:]\n    early_licks_all = trials['early_lick'][:]\n    instructions_all = trials['trial_instruction'][:]\n    photostim_onset_all = trials['photostim_onset'][:]\n    photostim_duration_all = trials['photostim_duration'][:]\n    trial_starts_all = trials['start_time'][:]\n\n    # --- Filter good units ---\n    classifications = units['classification'][:]\n    good_idx = np.where(classifications == 'good')[0]\n\n    if len(good_idx) == 0:\n        io.close()\n        return None\n\n    # --- Determine valid trials based on obs_intervals ---\n    # Use the obs_intervals from the first good unit (all units in a session\n    # share the same obs_intervals since they come from the same probe set)\n    obs_intervals = units['obs_intervals'][int(good_idx[0])]\n    valid_trial_mask = get_valid_trial_mask(obs_intervals, go_cue_times_all)\n\n    # Filter to valid trials only\n    valid_idx = np.where(valid_trial_mask)[0]\n    if len(valid_idx) < 2:\n        io.close()\n        return None\n\n    go_cue_times = go_cue_times_all[valid_idx]\n    outcomes = outcomes_all[valid_idx]\n    early_licks = early_licks_all[valid_idx]\n    instructions = instructions_all[valid_idx]\n    photostim_onset = photostim_onset_all[valid_idx]\n    photostim_duration = photostim_duration_all[valid_idx]\n    trial_starts = trial_starts_all[valid_idx]\n    n_trials = len(valid_idx)\n\n    # --- Session selection criteria (on valid trials only) ---\n    is_control = np.array([po == 'N/A' for po in photostim_onset])\n    is_no_early = early_licks == 'no early'\n    is_responded = outcomes != 'ignore'\n    control_responded = is_control & is_no_early & is_responded\n\n    if np.sum(control_responded) == 0:\n        io.close()\n        return None\n\n    n_correct_control = np.sum((outcomes == 'hit') & is_control & is_no_early)\n    performance = n_correct_control / np.sum(control_responded)\n\n    correct_left = np.sum((outcomes == 'hit') & (instructions == 'left') & is_control & is_no_early)\n    correct_right = np.sum((outcomes == 'hit') & (instructions == 'right') & is_control & is_no_early)\n\n    if performance < MIN_PERFORMANCE:\n        io.close()\n        return None\n    if correct_left < MIN_CORRECT_PER_SIDE or correct_right < MIN_CORRECT_PER_SIDE:\n        io.close()\n        return None\n\n    # --- Get brain regions ---\n    regions = []\n    for idx in good_idx:\n        eg = units['electrode_group'][int(idx)]\n        regions.append(get_brain_region(eg))\n\n    # --- Compute firing rates ---\n    spike_times_list = [units['spike_times'][int(idx)] for idx in good_idx]\n    fr_all = compute_firing_rates_all(spike_times_list, go_cue_times)\n\n    # --- Tone onset per trial ---\n    sorted_ss = np.sort(sample_start_times)\n    tone_onset_rel_go = np.full(n_trials, -1.85)\n    for t_i in range(n_trials):\n        go = go_cue_times[t_i]\n        # Find the original trial index to get the previous go cue correctly\n        orig_idx = valid_idx[t_i]\n        prev_go = go_cue_times_all[orig_idx - 1] if orig_idx > 0 else 0\n        i_lo = np.searchsorted(sorted_ss, prev_go, side='right')\n        i_hi = np.searchsorted(sorted_ss, go, side='right')\n        if i_hi > i_lo:\n            tone_onset_rel_go[t_i] = sorted_ss[i_hi - 1] - go\n\n    # --- Photostim binary per trial ---\n    photostim_on_all = np.zeros((n_trials, N_BINS), dtype=np.float32)\n    for t_i in range(n_trials):\n        if photostim_onset[t_i] != 'N/A':\n            ps_onset_abs = trial_starts[t_i] + float(photostim_onset[t_i])\n            ps_dur = float(photostim_duration[t_i])\n            ps_onset_rel = ps_onset_abs - go_cue_times[t_i]\n            ps_offset_rel = ps_onset_rel + ps_dur\n            photostim_on_all[t_i] = ((BIN_CENTERS >= ps_onset_rel) & (BIN_CENTERS < ps_offset_rel)).astype(np.float32)\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

bts = nwb.acquisition.get('BehavioralTimeSeries', None)\n    has_tongue = (bts is not None and 'Camera0_side_TongueTracking' in bts.time_series)\n\n    if has_tongue:\n        tongue_ts = data ---\n    outcome_map = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\n\n    neural_trials = []\n    input_trials = []\n    output_trials = []\n\n    for t_i in range(n_trials):\n        neural_trials.append(fr_all[:, t_i, :])\n\n        time_from_tone = (BIN_CENTERS - tone_onset_rel_go[t_i]).astype(np.float32)\n        inp = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim_on_all[t_i]], axis=0)\n The surviving values are averaged into 50 ms bins across the whole session, and the 40th and 60th percentiles of *those bin means* give two class edges. Each trial's window is then binned the same way and digitised against those edges, giving classes 0/1/2. The tongue is visible in only ~10% of frames, and when it is retracted the tracker still emits a position, so those frames are discarded rather than averaged in — including them shifts the percentiles and mixes real protrusions with noise. On the ~3% of trials where the go cue falls less than 2.5 s after trial start, the leading bins therefore contain no frames at all and fall into the `'not visible'` class.\n\n## 9. The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\n     4→quency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\n     5→response trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\n     6→\n     7→Two CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\n     8→\n     9→Multi-regional electrophysiological recordings\n    10→\n    11→We designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\n    12→\n    13→Extracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\n    14→\n    15→Probes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). we read an Nx1 MATLAB cell into a length-N Python list/array/anything iterable (and potentially do something before storing it into an iterable, e.g. if the cell contains indices, we might need to subtract 1 from each element since MATLAB counts from 1 and Python counts from 0), pay attention to the following three cases:\n    23→1. Empty 0x1 cell. Need to manually define what it becomes in Python.\n    24→2. 1x1 cell. Will be directly read as non-iterables such as str, int, float, etc. Need to manually convert it to an iterable with length 1.\n    25→3. Nx1 cell (N >= 2). Usually it's fine to read and directly store it in Python.\n    26→\n    27→Comment: in case 2, when using isinstance(data, data_type), if data could be a number, consider the possibility of data being either float or int, i.e. use\n    28→    if isinstance(data, float) or isinstance(data, int):\n    29→        do something\n    30→\"\"\"\n    31→\n    32→plt.rcParams['pdf.fonttype'] = 42\n    33→plt.rcParams['ps.fonttype'] = 42\n    34→\n    35→\n    36→def sliding_histogram(spikeTimes, begin_time, end_time, bin_width, stride, rate=True):\n    37→    '''\n    38→    Calculates the number of spikes for each unit in each sliding bin of width bin_width, strided by stride.\n    39→    begin_time and end_time are treated as the lower- and upper- bounds for bin centers.\n    40→    if rate=True, calculate firing rates instead of bin spikes.\n    41→    '''\n    42→    bin_begin_time = begin_time\n    43→    bin_end_time = end_time\n    44→    # This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    45→    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n    46→        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    47→    else:\n    48→        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    49→    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n    50→\n    51→    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n    52→\n    53→    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    54→    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n    55→        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n    56→\n    57→    if rate:\n    58→        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\n    59→    return binCenters, binSpikes\n    60→\n    61→\n    62→def process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0,
